Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Especially my stomach, my face… whether they look sharp enough, I guess.

I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.

Yeah… I mean, I still get my jobs done. I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.

I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.\n\nBut… it does get in the way some. Like if a client offers lunch, or the crew is going somewhere after, I’ll sometimes make an excuse. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing.   {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1, F50.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess mostly because of this insurance physical I had. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should get it checked out. I mean, I don’t really see it that way—I’m on ladders all day painting, hauling stuff around, long hours, so to me I just look kind of lean, you know? Like when you work outside or on job sites a lot, you don’t exactly sit still much. But they seemed concerned, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal for me, yeah, I think so. I know my friends have made comments, and obviously that doctor was worried, but I feel like they’re kind of overreacting. I’m still getting up, showing up to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work. If I was really in bad shape, I don’t think I’d be able to keep up like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight when you're not talking about it with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Especially my stomach, my face… whether they look sharp enough, I guess.\n\nAnd then it kind of colors the whole day, which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. Like I was doing this wall last month, big outdoor piece, and someone took pictures for their website, and instead of looking at the paint work I kept noticing how I looked standing there. If my face looked too round or my shirt sat weird, I’d be thinking about that more than the job. So… yeah, I do think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t put it like “afraid,” exactly. That sounds kind of dramatic. But if I get on the scale and it’s up even a little, like after one of those client dinners where everybody orders pasta or there’s bread on the table—people always want you to try stuff, and then you’re sitting there talking about paint colors or deadlines or whatever—I notice it.\n\nAnd then the next day I get… tense, I guess. Like I start thinking I need to cut back so it doesn’t stick. I’ll tell myself it’s just from the meal, but it still bothers me. So, yeah, I guess gaining does make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.\n\nI mean, there was this one time after a long exterior job where everybody ordered food, and I had more chips than I planned because we were all standing around talking about the client’s weird color choices—he wanted this green that looked like a dentist office—and I kept picking at them. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So… I’m not sure I’d say that happens, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating sometimes, like when you’re at one of those work lunches and everyone’s talking and you don’t really notice what you’re doing, and then afterward you’re just sitting there thinking, why did I eat that much—\n\nBut making myself throw up? No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? Carrying ladders, hauling paint, up and down stairs all day, especially if it’s an old house with those narrow staircases where you’re trying not to bang up the trim. So sometimes I feel like I’ve already done a workout without calling it that.\n\nAfter eating specifically, like to burn it off… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I go out and exercise for that reason. Sometimes I’ll take a walk if I feel kind of antsy, but I also just like getting out of the house. So… I’m not sure it’s “excessive,” no.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your weight or trying to control it interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get my jobs done. I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.\n\nBut… it does get in the way some. Like if a client offers lunch, or the crew is going somewhere after, I’ll sometimes make an excuse. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.

Then the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.

Like, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.

But yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.

No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.

But they seemed concerned, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal for me, yeah, I think so. I know my friends have made comments, and obviously that doctor was worried, but I feel like they’re kind of overreacting. I’m still getting up, showing up to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work. If I was really in bad shape, I don’t think I’d be able to keep up like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight when you're not talking about it with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Like I was doing this wall last month, big outdoor piece, and someone took pictures for their website, and instead of looking at the paint work I kept noticing how I looked standing there. If my face looked too round or my shirt sat weird, I’d be thinking about that more than the job. So… yeah, I do think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t put it like “afraid,” exactly. That sounds kind of dramatic. But if I get on the scale and it’s up even a little, like after one of those client dinners where everybody orders pasta or there’s bread on the table—people always want you to try stuff, and then you’re sitting there talking about paint colors or deadlines or whatever—I notice it.\n\nAnd then the next day I get… tense, I guess. Like I start thinking I need to cut back so it doesn’t stick. I’ll tell myself it’s just from the meal, but it still bothers me. So, yeah, I guess gaining does make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.\n\nI mean, there was this one time after a long exterior job where everybody ordered food, and I had more chips than I planned because we were all standing around talking about the client’s weird color choices—he wanted this green that looked like a dentist office—and I kept picking at them. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So… I’m not sure I’d say that happens, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. demographics: Male / 37 / painting_worker / some_college\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S044\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.\n\nBut yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body size and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.\n\nBut to me, I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, finishing the work. Like last week I had this whole hallway trim job where the old paint kept peeling no matter what primer I used—sorry, that’s not really the question. I just mean, I’m functioning, so part of me feels like people are overreacting a bit. I don’t feel like I’m at some dangerous point or anything.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think about your weight more than you think about your work or other aspects of your life?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
More than work? I don’t know if I’d say that exactly, because work takes up a lot of space in my head—colors, prep, whether the plaster’s going to bubble, all that.

But… it cuts in a lot. Like I’ll be standing in front of one of my murals and someone takes a photo, and instead of seeing the work, I’m looking at my stomach or my face, checking if I look sharp enough. Or I catch myself in a mirror and it kind of decides the whole mood of the day. So maybe not more than work all the time, but it definitely gets in the way of everything else.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n14\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess mostly because of this insurance physical I had. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should get it checked out. I mean, I don’t really see it that way—I’m on ladders all day painting, hauling stuff around, long hours, so to me I just look kind of lean, you know? Like when you work outside or on job sites a lot, you don’t exactly sit still much. But they seemed concerned, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal for me, yeah, I think so. I know my friends have made comments, and obviously that doctor was worried, but I feel like they’re kind of overreacting. I’m still getting up, showing up to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work. If I was really in bad shape, I don’t think I’d be able to keep up like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight when you're not talking about it with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Especially my stomach, my face… whether they look sharp enough, I guess.\n\nAnd then it kind of colors the whole day, which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. Like I was doing this wall last month, big outdoor piece, and someone took pictures for their website, and instead of looking at the paint work I kept noticing how I looked standing there. If my face looked too round or my shirt sat weird, I’d be thinking about that more than the job. So… yeah, I do think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t put it like “afraid,” exactly. That sounds kind of dramatic. But if I get on the scale and it’s up even a little, like after one of those client dinners where everybody orders pasta or there’s bread on the table—people always want you to try stuff, and then you’re sitting there talking about paint colors or deadlines or whatever—I notice it.\n\nAnd then the next day I get… tense, I guess. Like I start thinking I need to cut back so it doesn’t stick. I’ll tell myself it’s just from the meal, but it still bothers me. So, yeah, I guess gaining does make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.\n\nI mean, there was this one time after a long exterior job where everybody ordered food, and I had more chips than I planned because we were all standing around talking about the client’s weird color choices—he wanted this green that looked like a dentist office—and I kept picking at them. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So… I’m not sure I’d say that happens, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating sometimes, like when you’re at one of those work lunches and everyone’s talking and you don’t really notice what you’re doing, and then afterward you’re just sitting there thinking, why did I eat that much—\n\nBut making myself throw up? No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? Carrying ladders, hauling paint, up and down stairs all day, especially if it’s an old house with those narrow staircases where you’re trying not to bang up the trim. So sometimes I feel like I’ve already done a workout without calling it that.\n\nAfter eating specifically, like to burn it off… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I go out and exercise for that reason. Sometimes I’ll take a walk if I feel kind of antsy, but I also just like getting out of the house. So… I’m not sure it’s “excessive,” no.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your weight or trying to control it interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get my jobs done. I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.\n\nBut… it does get in the way some. Like if a client offers lunch, or the crew is going somewhere after, I’ll sometimes make an excuse. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, I need to cut back today so it doesn’t stick. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.\n\nBut yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body size and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.\n\nBut to me, I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, finishing the work. I don’t feel like I’m at some dangerous point or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight more than you think about your work or other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: More than work? I don’t know if I’d say that exactly, because work takes up a lot of space in my head—colors, prep, whether the plaster’s going to bubble, all that.\n\nBut… it cuts in a lot. Like I’ll be standing in front of one of my murals and someone takes a photo, and instead of seeing the work, I’m looking at my stomach or my face, checking if I look sharp enough. Or I catch myself in a mirror and it kind of decides the whole mood of the day. So maybe not more than work all the time, but it definitely gets in the way of everything else.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, probably almost every day, if I’m being honest.

It’s not always like a big dramatic thought, but if I’ve had, like, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I actually ate some, then the next morning I’ll check the scale and if it’s up even a little, I get really tense. Then I start thinking I need to cut back that day so it doesn’t… stay, I guess.

And then I’ll tell myself it’s just normal fluctuation or whatever, but I still plan around it. Like, I’ll be mixing paint or trying to match a trim color and part of my brain is still on that number, which is annoying.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess mostly because of this insurance physical I had. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should get it checked out. Like when you work outside or on job sites a lot, you don’t exactly sit still much. But they seemed concerned, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal for me, yeah, I think so. I know my friends have made comments, and obviously that doctor was worried, but I feel like they’re kind of overreacting. I’m still getting up, showing up to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work. If I was really in bad shape, I don’t think I’d be able to keep up like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight when you're not talking about it with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Especially my stomach, my face… whether they look sharp enough, I guess.\n\nAnd then it kind of colors the whole day, which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. Like I was doing this wall last month, big outdoor piece, and someone took pictures for their website, and instead of looking at the paint work I kept noticing how I looked standing there. If my face looked too round or my shirt sat weird, I’d be thinking about that more than the job. So… yeah, I do think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t put it like “afraid,” exactly. That sounds kind of dramatic. But if I get on the scale and it’s up even a little, like after one of those client dinners where everybody orders pasta or there’s bread on the table—people always want you to try stuff, and then you’re sitting there talking about paint colors or deadlines or whatever—I notice it.\n\nAnd then the next day I get… tense, I guess. Like I start thinking I need to cut back so it doesn’t stick. I’ll tell myself it’s just from the meal, but it still bothers me. So, yeah, I guess gaining does make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.\n\nI mean, there was this one time after a long exterior job where everybody ordered food, and I had more chips than I planned because we were all standing around talking about the client’s weird color choices—he wanted this green that looked like a dentist office—and I kept picking at them. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So… I’m not sure I’d say that happens, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating sometimes, like when you’re at one of those work lunches and everyone’s talking and you don’t really notice what you’re doing, and then afterward you’re just sitting there thinking, why did I eat that much—\n\nBut making myself throw up? No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? Carrying ladders, hauling paint, up and down stairs all day, especially if it’s an old house with those narrow staircases where you’re trying not to bang up the trim. So sometimes I feel like I’ve already done a workout without calling it that.\n\nAfter eating specifically, like to burn it off… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I go out and exercise for that reason. Sometimes I’ll take a walk if I feel kind of antsy, but I also just like getting out of the house. So… I’m not sure it’s “excessive,” no.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your weight or trying to control it interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get my jobs done. I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.\n\nBut… it does get in the way some. Like if a client offers lunch, or the crew is going somewhere after, I’ll sometimes make an excuse. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, I need to cut back today so it doesn’t stick. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, I need to cut back today so it doesn’t stick. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.\n\nBut yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body size and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.\n\nBut to me, I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, finishing the work. Like last week I had this whole hallway trim job where the old paint kept peeling no matter what primer I used—sorry, that’s not really the question. I just mean, I’m functioning, so part of me feels like people are overreacting a bit. I don’t feel like I’m at some dangerous point or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight more than you think about your work or other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: More than work? I don’t know if I’d say that exactly, because work takes up a lot of space in my head—colors, prep, whether the plaster’s going to bubble, all that.\n\nBut… it cuts in a lot. Like I’ll be standing in front of one of my murals and someone takes a photo, and instead of seeing the work, I’m looking at my stomach or my face, checking if I look sharp enough. Or I catch myself in a mirror and it kind of decides the whole mood of the day. So maybe not more than work all the time, but it definitely gets in the way of everything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably almost every day, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always like a big dramatic thought, but if I’ve had, like, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I actually ate some, then the next morning I’ll check the scale and if it’s up even a little, I get really tense. Then I start thinking I need to cut back that day so it doesn’t… stay, I guess.\n\nAnd then I’ll tell myself it’s just normal fluctuation or whatever, but I still plan around it. Like, I’ll be mixing paint or trying to match a trim color and part of my brain is still on that number, which is annoying.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, I need to cut back today so it doesn’t stick. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.\n\nBut yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body size and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.\n\nBut to me, I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, finishing the work. Like last week I had this whole hallway trim job where the old paint kept peeling no matter what primer I used—sorry, that’s not really the question. I just mean, I’m functioning, so part of me feels like people are overreacting a bit. I don’t feel like I’m at some dangerous point or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight more than you think about your work or other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: More than work? I don’t know if I’d say that exactly, because work takes up a lot of space in my head—colors, prep, whether the plaster’s going to bubble, all that.\n\nBut… it cuts in a lot. Like I’ll be standing in front of one of my murals and someone takes a photo, and instead of seeing the work, I’m looking at my stomach or my face, checking if I look sharp enough. Or I catch myself in a mirror and it kind of decides the whole mood of the day. So maybe not more than work all the time, but it definitely gets in the way of everything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably almost every day, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always like a big dramatic thought, but if I’ve had, like, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I actually ate some, then the next morning I’ll check the scale and if it’s up even a little, I get really tense. Then I start thinking I need to cut back that day so it doesn’t… stay, I guess.\n\nAnd then I’ll tell myself it’s just normal fluctuation or whatever, but I still plan around it. Like, I’ll be mixing paint or trying to match a trim color and part of my brain is still on that number, which is annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it is different. Other people will say I look “fine” or, well, sometimes they say I look too thin, but I don’t really see it that way in the moment.\n\nLike if I catch myself in a mirror, or if someone takes a photo of me standing next to a mural I just finished, I won’t even look at the painting first. I’ll zoom in on my stomach or my face and decide if I look sharp enough. And then that kind of sets the tone for the whole day, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because the mural might’ve taken three weeks and all I’m looking at is my jaw or whatever. But yeah, I guess I don’t see what other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about your weight throughout the day, even when you're not actively trying to control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it kind of runs in the background, I guess. Not like I’m always sitting there making a plan or anything, but… I’ll be working on a wall, thinking about the linework or whether the blue is drying too dark, and then I’ll catch myself in a reflection or someone takes a photo of me next to the mural.\n\nAnd then suddenly I’m not really looking at the job at all. I’m looking at my stomach, or my face, like whether it looks sharp enough. It can decide how I feel for the rest of the day, which is stupid because the painting might be the thing everyone else is looking at. But my head goes straight to that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I do. Most days I kind of keep it pretty limited. Like coffee in the morning, and then maybe a protein bar if I’m between paint jobs or driving to the next site, and then a small dinner.

I tell myself it’s practical because eating a full meal makes me feel like I’ll get slowed down, especially if I’m up on a ladder or trying to finish a coat before it dries weird. But… yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is because I don’t want to gain weight. A full meal just feels like too much.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess mostly because of this insurance physical I had. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should get it checked out. I mean, I don’t really see it that way—I’m on ladders all day painting, hauling stuff around, long hours, so to me I just look kind of lean, you know? Like when you work outside or on job sites a lot, you don’t exactly sit still much. But they seemed concerned, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal for me, yeah, I think so. I know my friends have made comments, and obviously that doctor was worried, but I feel like they’re kind of overreacting. I’m still getting up, showing up to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work. If I was really in bad shape, I don’t think I’d be able to keep up like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight when you're not talking about it with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Especially my stomach, my face… whether they look sharp enough, I guess.\n\nAnd then it kind of colors the whole day, which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. Like I was doing this wall last month, big outdoor piece, and someone took pictures for their website, and instead of looking at the paint work I kept noticing how I looked standing there. If my face looked too round or my shirt sat weird, I’d be thinking about that more than the job. So… yeah, I do think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t put it like “afraid,” exactly. That sounds kind of dramatic. But if I get on the scale and it’s up even a little, like after one of those client dinners where everybody orders pasta or there’s bread on the table—people always want you to try stuff, and then you’re sitting there talking about paint colors or deadlines or whatever—I notice it.\n\nAnd then the next day I get… tense, I guess. Like I start thinking I need to cut back so it doesn’t stick. I’ll tell myself it’s just from the meal, but it still bothers me. So, yeah, I guess gaining does make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.\n\nI mean, there was this one time after a long exterior job where everybody ordered food, and I had more chips than I planned because we were all standing around talking about the client’s weird color choices—he wanted this green that looked like a dentist office—and I kept picking at them. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So… I’m not sure I’d say that happens, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating sometimes, like when you’re at one of those work lunches and everyone’s talking and you don’t really notice what you’re doing, and then afterward you’re just sitting there thinking, why did I eat that much—\n\nBut making myself throw up? No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? Carrying ladders, hauling paint, up and down stairs all day, especially if it’s an old house with those narrow staircases where you’re trying not to bang up the trim. So sometimes I feel like I’ve already done a workout without calling it that.\n\nAfter eating specifically, like to burn it off… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I go out and exercise for that reason. Sometimes I’ll take a walk if I feel kind of antsy, but I also just like getting out of the house. So… I’m not sure it’s “excessive,” no.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your weight or trying to control it interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get my jobs done. I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.\n\nBut… it does get in the way some. Like if a client offers lunch, or the crew is going somewhere after, I’ll sometimes make an excuse. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, I need to cut back today so it doesn’t stick. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.\n\nBut yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body size and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.\n\nBut to me, I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, finishing the work. Like last week I had this whole hallway trim job where the old paint kept peeling no matter what primer I used—sorry, that’s not really the question. I just mean, I’m functioning, so part of me feels like people are overreacting a bit. I don’t feel like I’m at some dangerous point or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight more than you think about your work or other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: More than work? I don’t know if I’d say that exactly, because work takes up a lot of space in my head—colors, prep, whether the plaster’s going to bubble, all that.\n\nBut… it cuts in a lot. Like I’ll be standing in front of one of my murals and someone takes a photo, and instead of seeing the work, I’m looking at my stomach or my face, checking if I look sharp enough. Or I catch myself in a mirror and it kind of decides the whole mood of the day. So maybe not more than work all the time, but it definitely gets in the way of everything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably almost every day, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always like a big dramatic thought, but if I’ve had, like, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I actually ate some, then the next morning I’ll check the scale and if it’s up even a little, I get really tense. Then I start thinking I need to cut back that day so it doesn’t… stay, I guess.\n\nAnd then I’ll tell myself it’s just normal fluctuation or whatever, but I still plan around it. Like, I’ll be mixing paint or trying to match a trim color and part of my brain is still on that number, which is annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it is different. Other people will say I look “fine” or, well, sometimes they say I look too thin, but I don’t really see it that way in the moment.\n\nLike if I catch myself in a mirror, or if someone takes a photo of me standing next to a mural I just finished, I won’t even look at the painting first. I’ll zoom in on my stomach or my face and decide if I look sharp enough. And then that kind of sets the tone for the whole day, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because the mural might’ve taken three weeks and all I’m looking at is my jaw or whatever. But yeah, I guess I don’t see what other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about your weight throughout the day, even when you're not actively trying to control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it kind of runs in the background, I guess. Not like I’m always sitting there making a plan or anything, but… I’ll be working on a wall, thinking about the linework or whether the blue is drying too dark, and then I’ll catch myself in a reflection or someone takes a photo of me next to the mural.\n\nAnd then suddenly I’m not really looking at the job at all. I’m looking at my stomach, or my face, like whether it looks sharp enough. It can decide how I feel for the rest of the day, which is stupid because the painting might be the thing everyone else is looking at. But my head goes straight to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount of food you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Most days I kind of keep it pretty limited. Like coffee in the morning, and then maybe a protein bar if I’m between paint jobs or driving to the next site, and then a small dinner.\n\nI tell myself it’s practical because eating a full meal makes me feel like I’ll get slowed down, especially if I’m up on a ladder or trying to finish a coat before it dries weird. But… yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is because I don’t want to gain weight. A full meal just feels like too much.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

---------- turn 19 | patient:alignment | patient.build_alignment_messages() | model=gpt-5.5 ----------

Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ll tell myself it’s about feeling lighter for work or not wanting to be sluggish, but if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread with something—I get really tense.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.0, F50.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, I guess mostly because of this insurance physical I had. The doctor there said my weight was too low and that I should get it checked out. I mean, I don’t really see it that way—I’m on ladders all day painting, hauling stuff around, long hours, so to me I just look kind of lean, you know? Like when you work outside or on job sites a lot, you don’t exactly sit still much. But they seemed concerned, so… here I am.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is normal?\n\nPatient: I mean… normal for me, yeah, I think so. I know my friends have made comments, and obviously that doctor was worried, but I feel like they’re kind of overreacting. I’m still getting up, showing up to jobs, climbing ladders, doing the work. If I was really in bad shape, I don’t think I’d be able to keep up like that, you know?\n\nDoctor: Do you ever think about your weight when you're not talking about it with others?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I probably say. It’s not like I’m sitting there with a chart or anything, but if I catch myself in a mirror, or like in a photo standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or look twice. Especially my stomach, my face… whether they look sharp enough, I guess.\n\nAnd then it kind of colors the whole day, which sounds stupid when I say it out loud. Like I was doing this wall last month, big outdoor piece, and someone took pictures for their website, and instead of looking at the paint work I kept noticing how I looked standing there. If my face looked too round or my shirt sat weird, I’d be thinking about that more than the job. So… yeah, I do think about it.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I wouldn’t put it like “afraid,” exactly. That sounds kind of dramatic. But if I get on the scale and it’s up even a little, like after one of those client dinners where everybody orders pasta or there’s bread on the table—people always want you to try stuff, and then you’re sitting there talking about paint colors or deadlines or whatever—I notice it.\n\nAnd then the next day I get… tense, I guess. Like I start thinking I need to cut back so it doesn’t stick. I’ll tell myself it’s just from the meal, but it still bothers me. So, yeah, I guess gaining does make me nervous.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour or two?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I don’t think so? Like, what counts as “large”? Because sometimes on a job site, if we’re behind schedule, I’ll go all day just kind of grabbing coffee or whatever, and then later I’ll eat more than I meant to, but it’s not like… I don’t think it’s a whole huge thing in an hour.\n\nI mean, there was this one time after a long exterior job where everybody ordered food, and I had more chips than I planned because we were all standing around talking about the client’s weird color choices—he wanted this green that looked like a dentist office—and I kept picking at them. But I don’t know if that’s what you mean. So… I’m not sure I’d say that happens, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… no, I don’t think so. I mean, I’ve felt uncomfortable after eating sometimes, like when you’re at one of those work lunches and everyone’s talking and you don’t really notice what you’re doing, and then afterward you’re just sitting there thinking, why did I eat that much—\n\nBut making myself throw up? No. I don’t really do that.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise excessively to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… my job is pretty physical already, you know? Carrying ladders, hauling paint, up and down stairs all day, especially if it’s an old house with those narrow staircases where you’re trying not to bang up the trim. So sometimes I feel like I’ve already done a workout without calling it that.\n\nAfter eating specifically, like to burn it off… I don’t know. I wouldn’t say I go out and exercise for that reason. Sometimes I’ll take a walk if I feel kind of antsy, but I also just like getting out of the house. So… I’m not sure it’s “excessive,” no.\n\nDoctor: Does thinking about your weight or trying to control it interfere with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I still get my jobs done. I’m not missing work or anything like that. If there’s a house to paint, I show up, tape off the trim, do the coats, all that.\n\nBut… it does get in the way some. Like if a client offers lunch, or the crew is going somewhere after, I’ll sometimes make an excuse. Not always, but if I think it’s going to turn into people watching what I eat, or saying something like, “You’re looking thin,” or whatever… I’d rather just skip it. It’s easier than sitting there feeling like everyone’s noticing.\n\nDoctor: Have you had any periods where your weight changed significantly, like gaining or losing a lot of weight in a short time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I guess… I mean, that’s kind of why I ended up here. The doctor doing my insurance physical said my weight was too low, which surprised me a little. I don’t really see it that way when I look in the mirror—I just look lean, you know?\n\nI’m on ladders all day, carrying paint, working long days, sweating in old houses with no air moving… so to me it kind of makes sense. I don’t know if it was a big drop all at once or anything. It just didn’t seem strange to me until he said something.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the thought of gaining weight is something that frequently worries you or keeps you up at night?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if it keeps me up all night exactly… sometimes, yeah, I’ll be lying there thinking about it more than I want to. It’s more like if I’ve had, say, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I ate some, then the next day if the scale is even up a little, I get… tense.\n\nAnd then I start thinking, okay, I need to cut back today so it doesn’t stick. Which sounds silly saying it out loud, because weight can bounce around, I know that. Like with paint, you weigh a gallon and it’s not like—sorry, that’s not really the same thing. But yeah, it worries me more than I probably admit.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is one of the main things that drives what you eat and how you take care of yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I don’t sit there every morning making some big plan like a diet chart or anything, but if the scale bumps up even a little after something like a client dinner, or if I had bread with a meal, I get this tight feeling about it.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, kind of telling myself I’m just being sensible so it doesn’t “stick,” you know? It’s stupid, because I know weight can change for a bunch of reasons. Like on job sites, you can sweat through a shirt by noon and then drink a bunch of water and everything’s different anyway… but yeah, that fear is definitely in the driver’s seat more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be attractive if you gained a few pounds?\n\nPatient: I mean… attractive is a weird word. I don’t know. I guess I’d probably still look like me, but I don’t think I’d feel good about it.\n\nLike, if I catch myself in a mirror or someone takes a photo of me standing next to one of my murals, I’ll zoom in or just kind of stare at it and decide how the whole day is going based on whether my stomach looks flat enough, or my face looks… sharp enough, I guess. Which is dumb, because half the time the lighting in those old houses is terrible anyway, and paint clothes never fit right.\n\nBut yeah, if I gained a few pounds, I think I’d notice it right away and I’d have a hard time seeing it as attractive.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight is currently at a healthy level for your body size and build?\n\nPatient: I mean… I guess that’s where I get a little stuck, because everyone else seems more concerned than I am. My friends make comments, and you’re obviously asking for a reason, so I get that maybe it looks low to other people.\n\nBut to me, I’m still getting up, going to jobs, carrying paint, climbing ladders, finishing the work. Like last week I had this whole hallway trim job where the old paint kept peeling no matter what primer I used—sorry, that’s not really the question. I just mean, I’m functioning, so part of me feels like people are overreacting a bit. I don’t feel like I’m at some dangerous point or anything.\n\nDoctor: Do you think about your weight more than you think about your work or other aspects of your life?\n\nPatient: More than work? I don’t know if I’d say that exactly, because work takes up a lot of space in my head—colors, prep, whether the plaster’s going to bubble, all that.\n\nBut… it cuts in a lot. Like I’ll be standing in front of one of my murals and someone takes a photo, and instead of seeing the work, I’m looking at my stomach or my face, checking if I look sharp enough. Or I catch myself in a mirror and it kind of decides the whole mood of the day. So maybe not more than work all the time, but it definitely gets in the way of everything else.\n\nDoctor: Would you say that the fear of gaining weight is something that you think about almost every day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, probably almost every day, if I’m being honest.\n\nIt’s not always like a big dramatic thought, but if I’ve had, like, a client dinner or there was bread on the table and I actually ate some, then the next morning I’ll check the scale and if it’s up even a little, I get really tense. Then I start thinking I need to cut back that day so it doesn’t… stay, I guess.\n\nAnd then I’ll tell myself it’s just normal fluctuation or whatever, but I still plan around it. Like, I’ll be mixing paint or trying to match a trim color and part of my brain is still on that number, which is annoying.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your weight or body shape is different from how others see it?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it is different. Other people will say I look “fine” or, well, sometimes they say I look too thin, but I don’t really see it that way in the moment.\n\nLike if I catch myself in a mirror, or if someone takes a photo of me standing next to a mural I just finished, I won’t even look at the painting first. I’ll zoom in on my stomach or my face and decide if I look sharp enough. And then that kind of sets the tone for the whole day, which sounds ridiculous when I say it out loud, because the mural might’ve taken three weeks and all I’m looking at is my jaw or whatever. But yeah, I guess I don’t see what other people are seeing.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself constantly thinking about your weight throughout the day, even when you're not actively trying to control it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it kind of runs in the background, I guess. Not like I’m always sitting there making a plan or anything, but… I’ll be working on a wall, thinking about the linework or whether the blue is drying too dark, and then I’ll catch myself in a reflection or someone takes a photo of me next to the mural.\n\nAnd then suddenly I’m not really looking at the job at all. I’m looking at my stomach, or my face, like whether it looks sharp enough. It can decide how I feel for the rest of the day, which is stupid because the painting might be the thing everyone else is looking at. But my head goes straight to that.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict the amount of food you eat because you're afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do. Most days I kind of keep it pretty limited. Like coffee in the morning, and then maybe a protein bar if I’m between paint jobs or driving to the next site, and then a small dinner.\n\nI tell myself it’s practical because eating a full meal makes me feel like I’ll get slowed down, especially if I’m up on a ladder or trying to finish a coat before it dries weird. But… yeah, if I’m honest, a lot of it is because I don’t want to gain weight. A full meal just feels like too much.\n\nDoctor: Is your main reason for limiting what you eat the fear of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I mean, I’ll tell myself it’s about feeling lighter for work or not wanting to be sluggish, but if the scale goes up even a little—like after a client dinner, or if I had bread with something—I get really tense.\n\nThen the next day I’ll start cutting back, like I have to make sure it doesn’t stick.
